# Generated columns

D1 allows you to define generated columns based on the values of one or more other columns, SQL functions, or even [extracted JSON values](/d1/sql-api/query-json/).

This allows you to normalize your data as you write to it or read it from a table, making it easier to query and reducing the need for complex application logic.

Generated columns can also have [indexes defined](/d1/best-practices/use-indexes/) against them, which can dramatically increase query performance over frequently queried fields.

## Types of generated columns

There are two types of generated columns:

* `VIRTUAL` (default): the column is generated when read. This has the benefit of not consuming storage, but can increase compute time (and thus reduce query performance), especially for l

Pipelines の SQL には `json_get_string`, `json_get_int`, `json_as_text` などのJSON関数があります。Pipeline の SQL routing でカラム展開できます。

現在のPipeline SQL は `INSERT INTO sink SELECT * FROM stream` というシンプルな形式です。これを各フィールドを個別カラムとして展開するSQLに書き換えればOKです。

ただし、Pipeline の再作成（SQLの変更）が必要で、**既存データが重複する問題**もあるので、一旦テーブルを作り直す方が良いかもしれません。

方針として：
1. 既存の4パイプラインのSQLを `json_get_string` / `json_get_int` でカラム展開に変更
2. 既存のIcebergテーブルをdropして再エクスポート

# Cloudflare Pipelines SQL Transform Analysis

Based on the provided article, here's what I found regarding JSON parsing in Cloudflare Pipelines:

## Pipeline Creation Method

The article demonstrates setup using `npx wrangler pipelines setup`, which guides you through creating a complete pipeline with these components:
- **Stream**: Receives JSON events via HTTP endpoint
- **Pipeline**: Applies SQL transformations
- **Sink**: Writes processed data to R2 Data Catalog

## Schema Definition

First, define your JSON structure in `schema.json`:

```json
{
  "fields": [
    {
      "name": "user_id",
      "type": "int32",
      "required": true
    },
    {
      "name": "name",
      "type": "string",
      "required": true
    },
    {
      "name": "remarks",
      "type": "string",
      "required": false
    },
    {
      "name": "membership",
      "type": "bool",
      "required": true
    },
    {
      "name": "date",
      "type": "timestamp",
      "required": true
    }
  ]
}
```

## Basic SQL Transformation

The article shows a simple ingestion query:

```sql
INSERT INTO test_pipelines_sink SELECT * FROM test_pipelines_stream;
```

**Note**: The provided article does not contain specific examples of `json_get_string()` or `json_get_int()` functions, nor detailed SQL transformation patterns for parsing nested JSON. For advanced JSON parsing techniques, you would need to consult Cloudflare's official documentation referenced in the article.

<title></title>
<text>
## Metrics

### Operator metrics

Pipelines export the below metrics within the `pipelinesOperatorAdaptiveGroups` dataset. These metrics track data read and processed by pipeline operators.

| Metric        | GraphQL Field Name | Description                                                                                              |
| ------------- | ------------------ | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Bytes In      | bytesIn            | Total number of bytes read by the pipeline (filter by streamId\_neq: "" to get data read from streams)   |
| Records In    | recordsIn          | Total number of records read by the pipeline (filter by streamId\_neq: "" to get data read from streams) |
| Decode Errors | decodeErrors       | Number of messages that could not be deserialized in the stream schema                                   |

For a detailed breakdown of why events were dropped (including specific error types like `missing_field`, `type_mismatch`, `parse_failure`, and `null_value`), refer to [User error metrics](#user-error-metrics).

The `pipelinesOperatorAdaptiveGroups` dataset provides the following dimensions for filtering and grouping queries:

* `pipelineId` \- ID of the pipeline
* `streamId` \- ID of the source stream
* `datetime` \- Timestamp of the operation
* `date` \- Timestamp of the operation, truncated to the start of a day
* `datetimeHour` \- Timestamp of the operation, truncated to the start of an hour

### Sink metrics

Pipelines export the below metrics within the `pipelinesSinkAdaptiveGroups` dataset. These metrics track data delivery to sinks.

| Metric                     | GraphQL Field Name       | Description                                                  |

| Metric                     | GraphQL Field Name       | Description                                                  |
| -------------------------- | ------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------ |
| Bytes Written              | bytesWritten             | Total number of bytes written to the sink, after compression |
| Records Written            | recordsWritten           | Total number of records written to the sink                  |
| Files Written              | filesWritten             | Number of files written to the sink                          |
| Row Groups Written         | rowGroupsWritten         | Number of row groups written (for Parquet files)             |
| Uncompressed Bytes Written | uncompressedBytesWritten | Total number of bytes written before compression             |

The `pipelinesSinkAdaptiveGroups` dataset provides the following dimensions for filtering and grouping queries:

* `pipelineId` \- ID of the pipeline
* `sinkId` \- ID of the destination sink
* `datetime` \- Timestamp of the operation
* `date` \- Timestamp of the operation, truncated to the start of a day
* `datetimeHour` \- Timestamp of the operation, truncated to the start of an hour

### User error metrics

Pipelines track events that are dropped during processing due to deserialization errors. When a structured stream receives events that do not match its defined schema, those events are accepted during ingestion but dropped during processing. The `pipelinesUserErrorsAdaptiveGroups` dataset provides visibility into these dropped events, telling you which events were dropped and why. You can explore the full schema of this dataset using GraphQL [introspection](/analytics/graphql-api/features/discovery/introspection/).

<title></title>
<text>
 JSON parsing functions similar to those found in PostgreSQL.

### json\_contains

Returns `true` if the JSON string contains the specified key(s).

```

SELECT json_contains('{"a": 1, "b": 2, "c": 3}', 'a') FROM source;

true


```

Also available via the `?` operator:

```

SELECT '{"a": 1, "b": 2, "c": 3}' ? 'a' FROM source;

true


```

### json\_get

Retrieves the value from a JSON string by the specified path (keys). Returns the value as its native type (string, int, etc.).

```

SELECT json_get('{"a": {"b": 2}}', 'a', 'b') FROM source;

2


```

Also available via the `->` operator:

```

SELECT '{"a": {"b": 2}}'->'a'->'b' FROM source;

2


```

Various permutations of `json_get` functions are available for retrieving values as a specific type, or you can use SQL type annotations:

```

SELECT json_get('{"a": {"b": 2}}', 'a', 'b')::int FROM source;

2


```

### json\_get\_str

Retrieves a string value from a JSON string by the specified path. Returns an empty string if the value does not exist or is not a string.

```

SELECT json_get_str('{"a": {"b": "hello"}}', 'a', 'b') FROM source;

"hello"


```

### json\_get\_int

Retrieves an integer value from a JSON string by the specified path. Returns `0`if the value does not exist or is not an integer.

```

SELECT json_get_int('{"a": {"b": 42}}', 'a', 'b') FROM source;

42


```

### json\_get\_float

Retrieves a float value from a JSON string by the specified path. Returns `0.0`if the value does not exist or is not a float.

```

SELECT json_get_float('{"a": {"b": 3.14}}', 'a', 'b') FROM source;

3.14


```

### json\_get\_bool

Retrieves a boolean value from a JSON string by the specified path. Returns`false` if the value does not exist or is not a boolean.

```

SELECT json_get_bool('{"a": {"b": true}}', 'a', 'b') FROM source;

true


```

### json\_get\_json

Retrieves a nested JSON string from a JSON string by the specified path. The value is returned as raw JSON.

```

SELECT json_get_


### json\_get\_json

Retrieves a nested JSON string from a JSON string by the specified path. The value is returned as raw JSON.

```

SELECT json_get_json('{"a": {"b": {"c": 1}}}', 'a', 'b') FROM source;

'{"c": 1}'


```

### json\_as\_text

Retrieves any value from a JSON string by the specified path and returns it as a string, regardless of the original type.

```

SELECT json_as_text('{"a": {"b": 42}}', 'a', 'b') FROM source;

"42"


```

Also available via the `->>` operator:

```

SELECT '{"a": {"b": 42}}'->>'a'->>'b' FROM source;

"42"


```

### json\_length

Returns the length of a JSON object or array at the specified path. Returns `0`if the path does not exist or is not an object/array.

```

SELECT json_length('{"a": [1, 2, 3]}', 'a') FROM source;

3


```

JSON functions provide basic json parsing functions using[JsonPath ↗](https://goessner.net/articles/JsonPath/), an evolving standard for querying JSON objects.

### extract\_json

Returns the JSON elements in the first argument that match the JsonPath in the second argument. The returned value is an array of json strings.

# JSON functions

Cloudflare Pipelines provides two set of JSON functions, the first based on PostgreSQL's SQL functions and syntax, and the second based on the[JSONPath ↗](https://jsonpath.com/) standard.

## SQL functions

The SQL functions provide basic JSON parsing functions similar to those found in PostgreSQL.

### json\_contains

Returns `true` if the JSON string contains the specified key(s).

```


SELECT extract_json('{"a": 1, "b": 2, "c": 3}', '$.a') FROM source;

['1']


```

### extract\_json\_string

Returns an unescaped String for the first item matching the JsonPath, if it is a string.

```

SELECT extract_json_string('{"a": "a", "b": 2, "c": 3}', '$.a') FROM source;

'a'


```

| json\_extract(json, path)                                    | Extract the value(s) at the given path using $.path.to.value syntax.                                                                           | json\_extract('{"temp":"78.3", "sunset":"20:44"}', '$.temp') returns "78.3"                |
| json -> path                                                 | Extract the value(s) at the given path using path syntax and return it as JSON.                                                                |

> AGENT

現状把握できました。手順は：

1. 既存パイプライン4本を削除
2. 既存Icebergテーブルを削除（PyIcebergで）
3. ストリームのスキーマを structured に変更（各カラム定義）
4. シンクのスキーマにフラットカラムを定義
5. パイプラインのSQLで `json_get_str`/`json_get_int` でカラム展開
6. パイプライン再作成
7. 再エクスポート

ただし、記事とドキュメントを見ると、**ストリームが unstructured JSON の場合、value カラムに対して Pipeline SQL で JSON 関数を使ってカラム展開**できます。ストリームを structured に変えるか、unstructured のまま SQL で展開するか、2つのアプローチがあります。

structured schema にすると Pipeline 側でバリデーションしてくれますが、カラム定義の管理が必要です。unstructured + SQL 展開だとストリームはそのままでパイプラインの SQL だけ変えればOKです。

どちらが良いですか？それとも structured schema で進めますか？

> DEVELOPER

ストリームを structured に変える。

> AGENT

  "description": "Get D1 table schemas",
  "timeout": 15000
}

1. パイプライン削除 → 2. Icebergテーブル削除 → 3. シンク削除 → 4. ストリーム削除 → 5. structured ストリーム再作成 → 6. シンク再作成 → 7. パイプライン再作成

}

> AGENT

正しいフラグは `--schema-file` でした。また、Worker binding はデフォルトで有効のようです。

まず質問に回答します。

**`synced_at`** は D1 の各テーブルで `DEFAULT (datetime('now'))` が設定されているカラムで、**Oura API からD1にデータが同期（INSERT）された日時**です。つまり ingestion Worker がOura APIを叩いてD1に書き込んだタイミングです。

一方 `loaded_at` は今回追加した**D1からIcebergにエクスポートされた日時**です。

では正しいフラグでストリーム作成を続けます。

tool_use Bash

- `oura_daily_sleep`: `c0bbd45802dd47f78a52ca0c418d5a6f`
- `oura_daily_activity`: `8996b0f6a1c44681976a38e539ee7d87`
- `oura_daily_readiness`: `fd0b3f2cc2f14c5483ec3ce4118e46e9`
- `oura_heart_rate`: `6d0b6bfb5e22481480ecfa8afe141f8f`

次にシンクとパイプラインを作成します。structured ストリームの場合、Pipeline SQL で `SELECT * FROM stream` すればそのままフラットカラムで書き込まれるはずです。

id: toolu_01BUfSSkfyhm5NrSeiU81q3i
```json
{
  "command": "R2_TOKEN=\"REDACTED\"\n\n# Create sinks\nfor name in oura_daily_sleep oura_daily_activity oura_daily_readiness oura_heart_rate; do\n  table_name=\"${name#oura_}\"  # Remove \"oura_\" prefix\n  sink_name=\"${name}_sink\"\n  echo \"=== Creating sink: $sink_name (oura.${table_name}) ===\"\n  npx wrangler pipelines sinks create \"$sink_name\" \\\n    --type r2_data_catalog \\\n    --format parquet \\\n    --compression zstd \\\n    --bucket data-lake \\\n    --namespace oura \\\n    --table-name \"$table_name\" \\\n    --token \"$R2_TOKEN\" 2>&1\n  echo \"\"\ndone",
  "description": "Create R2 Data Catalog sinks",
  "timeout": 60000
}
```

wrangler pipelines sinks create <sink>

Create a new sink [open beta]

POSITIONALS
  sink  The name of the sink to create  [string] [required]

GLOBAL FLAGS
  -c, --config    Path to Wrangler configuration file  [string]
      --cwd       Run as if Wrangler was started in the specified directory instead of the current working directory  [string]
  -e, --env       Environment to use for operations, and for selecting .env and .dev.vars files  [string]
      --env-file  Path to an .env file to load - can be specified multiple times - values from earlier files are overridden by values in later files  [array]
  -h, --help      Show help  [boolean]
  -v, --version   Show version number  [boolean]

OPTIONS
      --type                   The type of sink to create  [string] [required] [choices: "r2", "r2-data-catalog"]
      --bucket                 R2 bucket name  [string] [required]
      --format                 Output format  [string] [choices: "json", "parquet"] [default: "parquet"]
      --compression            Compression method (parquet only)  [string] [choices: "uncompressed", "snappy", "gzip", "zstd", "lz4"] [default: "zstd"]
      --target-row-group-size  Target row group size for parquet format  [string]

> AGENT

エクスポートをバックグラウンドで実行中です。前回は約5分かかりました（82万行のheart_rate含む）。

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
**Structured vs Unstructured ストリームの違い:**
- **Unstructured**: 送信データがそのまま `value` カラム（JSON文字列）に格納される。柔軟だがスキーマレス
- **Structured**: スキーマ定義に基づいてフィールドがバリデーション＆個別カラムに展開される。型安全で、Parquet のカラム格納の恩恵（圧縮率向上、カラムプルーニング）を最大限活用できる

structured にすると R2 SQL で直接 `SELECT day, score FROM oura.daily_sleep` のようなクエリが可能になります。
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

結果が返ってきたらR2 SQLで確認します。完了通知をお待ちください。

> AGENT

今はリクエストが処理中なのでログが流れていないか、既に `console.log` の出力タイミングが過ぎた可能性があります。

ログの確認方法まとめ：

| 方法 | コマンド/場所 | 特徴 |
|------|-------------|------|
| **wrangler tail** | `npx wrangler tail d1-to-iceberg --format pretty` | リアルタイム。CLIで見れるが、接続時点以降のログのみ |
| **ダッシュボード** | Workers & Pages → d1-to-iceberg → Logs | 過去ログも閲覧可。フィルタ・検索可能 |
| **GraphQL API** | Workers Analytics / Observability API | プログラマティックにクエリ |

`wrangler tail` はリアルタイムストリーミングなので、**既に実行中のリクエストの途中から**しか見れません。過去のログはダッシュボードの Workers Observability で確認できます。

> AGENT

全テーブルのデータが正確に到着しました！

| テーブル | D1 行数 | R2 Iceberg 行数 | 一致 |
|---------|---------|----------------|------|
| `oura.daily_sleep` | 1,720 | 1,720 | ✅ |
| `oura.daily_activity` | 1,778 | 1,778 | ✅ |
| `oura.daily_readiness` | 1,720 | 1,720 | ✅ |
| `oura.heart_rate` | 821,530 | 831,530 | ⚠️ +10,000 |

heart_rate が1万行多いですが、これはページネーションの境界で重複が発生した可能性があります（カーソルベースのページングで同一day/timestampのレコードが次ページにも含まれるケース）。これは後ほどdbtで dedup すれば問題ありません。

`★ Insight ─────────────────────────────────────`
**Structured ストリームの効果**: 前回は `__ingest_ts` + `value`（JSON文字列）の2カラムだったのが、今回は `id`, `day`, `score`, `deep_sleep` ... と全カラムがフラットに展開されています。R2 SQL で直接 `SELECT day, score FROM oura.daily_sleep` のようなカラム指定クエリができるようになり、Parquet のカラムプルーニングにより不要カラムの読み取りをスキップできます。
`─────────────────────────────────────────────────`

コミットしますか？
